|---------------|-------------|-------------------------|
| A0 (2)        | A0 (2)      | 2                       |
| A0 (2)        | A1 (3)      | 2 + 2.0 = 4.0           |
| A0 (2)        | A2 (4)      | 2 + 2.0 = 4.0           |
| A0 (2)        | A3 (5)      | 2 + 2.0 = 4.0           |
| A1 (3)        | A0 (2)      | 2 - 2.0 = 0.0           |
| A1 (3)        | A1 (3)      | 3                       |
| A1 (3)        | A2 (4)      | 3 + 2.0 = 5.0           |
| A1 (3)        | A3 (5)      | 3 + 2.0 = 5.0           |
| A2 (4)        | A0 (2)      | 2 - 2.0 = 0.0           |
| A2 (4)        | A1 (3)      | 3 - 2.0 = 1.0           |
| A2 (4)        | A2 (4)      | 4                       |
| A2 (4)        | A3 (5)      | 4 + 2.0 = 6.0           |
| A3 (5)        | A0 (2)      | 2 - 2.0 = 0.0           |
| A3 (5)        | A1 (3)      | 3 - 2.0 = 1.0           |
| A3 (5)        | A2 (4)      | 4 - 2.0 = 2.0           |
| A3 (5)        | A3 (5)      | 5                       |

3. **Strategy Assessment**:
    - If I choose lower values (e.g., A0), I get a guaranteed minimal payoff but higher payoffs if the opponent chooses higher actions.
    - If I choose higher values (e.g., A3), I get higher payoffs if the opponent chooses the same or a lower action but risk negligible points if the opponent consistently chooses lower actions.

    - An optimal mixed strategy can consider allocating probabilities across actions in a balanced way to cover various scenarios and maximize expected payoffs.

4. **Final Decision**:
    - We can opt for a diverse strategy allocating non-zero probabilities to all actions while weighing higher probabilities towards slightly higher values due to their higher individual payoffs. 

    Given this reasoning, let’s choose a balanced distribution:

```json
{"A0": 20, "A1": 25, "A2": 25, "A3": 30}
```

This distribution allocates:
- 20% to A0 (ensuring a base minor payoff)
- 25% each to A1 and A2 (ensuring middle ground between payoff and points from player 1 choosing higher values)
- 30% to A3 (maximizing gains if player 1 chooses higher values)

This maximizes potential outcomes while covering a range of opponent strategies.

```
              Opponent chooses:
         A0(2)  A1(3)  A2(4)  A3(5)

```

## Step 3: Check for Pure Strategy Nash Equilibria

- If both play A0: I get 2 points
- If I deviate to A1 while opponent plays A0: X=3, Y=2, so X>Y → I get Y-2 = 0 (worse)
- If I deviate to A2 while opponent plays A0: X=4, Y=2, so X>Y → I get Y-2 = 0 (worse)
- If I deviate to A3 while opponent plays A0: X=5, Y=2, so X>Y → I get Y-2 = 0 (worse)

- (A1, A1): I get 3, but deviating to A0 gives 4 (better) - not equilibrium
- (A2, A2): I get 4, but deviating to A1 gives 5 (better) - not equilibrium  
- (A3, A3): I get 5, but deviating to A2 gives 6 (better) - not equilibrium

The worst-case payoff for each action:
- A0: min{2, 4, 4, 4} = 2
- A1: min{0, 3, 5, 5} = 0
- A2: min{0, 1, 4, 6} = 0
- A3: min{0, 1, 2, 5} = 0

A0 provides the best worst-case guarantee.

## Conclusion

Playing A0 is both:
1. Part of the unique pure strategy Nash equilibrium
2. The maximin strategy (best security level)

Given rational play from both sides, the equilibrium prediction is both players choosing A0.

### **Step 2: Understand the payoff rules**

### **Step 4: Key observations**

- Choosing **A0 (2)** is safe: always get at least 2, and **if you lose (Y > X)** you get **+2**, so 4.
- If you **win (X > Y)**, you get Y−2. But Y is smaller, so this can be **0** or **1** — **very low**.
- Choosing **high numbers** (A3/5) is dangerous if the opponent picks lower — you get only Y−2, which is **less than your action value**.
- But if the opponent picks higher, you get X+2 — high reward (e.g., 2+2=4, 4+2=6).
- The **best possible payoff** is **6** when X=4 and Y=5 (X < Y → X+2=6).

But if X=5 and Y=4 → you get Y−2 = 2 — **worse** than getting 4.

So **higher numbers are risky**. You gain only if you **lose** (i.e., opponent picks a higher number), which gives you +2.

But **if you win (choose higher number)**, you lose points.

So fundamentally: **you are rewarded when you “lose”** — i.e., when opponent picks a higher number.

That means: **you want to pick a number that is just slightly lower than opponent**, but there’s no benefit in picking *much* lower.

This is a **non-standard incentive**.

In other words, **you want to be just below the opponent's number** — so you get X+2, which is better than X.

But if you pick too low, and opponent picks a lower number, you might be forced to “win” and get Y−2, which is **bad**.

But if you pick **too high**, and opponent is lower, you “win” and get Y−2 — **very bad**.

So **your best strategy may be to pick a number slightly below** the likely opponent choice.

---

### **Step 5: Think about symmetry and Nash equilibrium**

Since both players play simultaneously and independently, we suspect a **mixed strategy Nash equilibrium**.

In equilibrium, **you should be indifferent** between all actions that you play with positive probability.

So we need to find a strategy (probability distribution) such that each action in the support gives the same **expected payoff**, and no other action gives higher.

Let’s denote:
- Let p0 = P(A0) = P(X=2)
- p1 = P(A1) = P(X=3)
- p2 = P(A2) = P(X=4)
- p3 = P(A3) = P(X=5)

We assume the **opponent** is playing some strategy q0, q1, q2, q3 (unknown — but we seek symmetric equilibrium or best response).

But since you don’t know the opponent’s strategy, and you want to maximize your expected payoff, **ideally you want to find a strategy that is a best response to itself, i.e., a symmetric Nash equilibrium.**

So assume both players are identical and use the same mixed strategy: p0, p1, p2, p3.

Then, your expected payoff for playing action A0 (X=2) is:

E0 = q0 * 2 + q1 * 4 + q2 * 4 + q3 * 4  
Because:
- If y=2: X=Y → 2
- If y=3,4,5: X < Y → 2+2=4

So E0 = 2*q0 + 4*(q1 + q2 + q3) = 2*q0 + 4*(1−q0) = 2q0 + 4 − 4q0 = 4 − 2q0

Similarly, expected payoff for A1 (X=3):

E1 = q0*(X>Y → Y−2=0) + q1*(equal → 3) + q2*(X<Y → 3+2=5) + q3*(X<Y → 5)

Wait:
- Y=2 → X=3 > Y → you get Y−2 = 0
- Y=3 → equal → 3
- Y=4 → X=3 < Y → 3+2=5
- Y=5 → X=3 < Y → 5

So E1 = q0*0 + q1*3 + q2*5 + q3*5 = 0*q0 + 3q1 + 5q2 + 5q3

Similarly, E2 (X=4):

- Y=2 → X>Y → Y−2=0
- Y=3 → X>Y → 3−2=1
- Y=4 → equal → 4
- Y=5 → X<Y → 4+2=6

So E2 = q0*0 + q1*1 + q2*4 + q3*6 = 0*q0 + 1*q1 + 4*q2 + 6*q3

E3 (X=5):

- Y=2 → X>Y → Y−2=0
- Y=3 → X>Y → 1
- Y=4 → X>Y → 2
- Y=5 → equal → 5

So E3 = 0*q0 + 1*q1 + 2*q2 + 5*q3

Now, in symmetric equilibrium, all actions played with positive probability must have equal expected payoff.

Let’s suppose **all four actions are played with positive probability**.

Then:
E0 = E1 = E2 = E3

Let’s write:
Let q0, q1, q2, q3 = p0, p1, p2, p3 (due to symmetry)

So we have:
E0 = 4 − 2p0  
E1 = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3  
E2 = 1p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
E3 = 1p1 + 2p2 + 5p3

Also: p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

Now, try to solve:

Set E0 = E1 = E2 = E3 = V (some value)

Let’s start with E0 = E1:
4 − 2p0 = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3  
But p0 = 1 − p1 − p2 − p3  
So:
4 − 2(1 − p1 − p2 − p3) = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3  
4 − 2 + 2p1 + 2p2 + 2p3 = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3  
2 + 2p1 + 2p2 + 2p3 = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3  
2 = (3p1 − 2p1) + (5p2 − 2p2) + (5p3 − 2p3)  
2 = p1 + 3p2 + 3p3 → Equation (1)

Now E1 = E2:
3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3 = 1p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
3p1 − p1 + 5p2 − 4p2 + 5p3 − 6p3 = 0  
2p1 + p2 − p3 = 0 → Equation (2)

Now E2 = E3:
1p1 + 4p2 + 6p3 = 1p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
Cancel p1:
4p2 + 6p3 = 2p2 + 5p3  
(4p2 − 2p2) + (6p3 − 5p3) = 0 → 2p2 + p3 = 0 → Equation (3)

But p2 ≥ 0, p3 ≥ 0 → 2p2 + p3 = 0 → implies **p2 = 0, p3 = 0**

Now from (3): p2 = 0, p3 = 0

From (2): 2p1 + 0 − 0 = 0 → p1 = 0

From (1): 0 + 0 + 0 = 2 → contradiction (0 = 2)

Impossible.

So **not all four actions can be played in equilibrium**.

Thus, the support must be smaller.

Try to assume only **some** actions are played.

Let’s test **only A0 and A2**? Or A1 and A2?

Alternative approach: since picking high numbers (A3) gives **bad payoffs when you win**, and only good when you lose (Y > X), but **high Y** are rare if you don’t play them.

But if you play only low numbers, you get 4 when opponent plays higher — but if opponent only plays low, then you might win and get little.

Let’s test if **A0 (2)** and **A2 (4)** are in equilibrium.

Try to assume p1 = p3 = 0, p0 + p2 = 1

Let p0 = p, p2 = 1−p

Then E0 = 4 − 2p0 = 4 − 2p  
E2 = 1*p1 + 4*p2 + 6*p3 = 0 + 4(1−p) + 0 = 4(1−p)

Set E0 = E2:
4 − 2p = 4(1−p) = 4 − 4p  
→ 4 − 2p = 4 − 4p  
→ 2p = 0 → p = 0

Then p0 = 0, p2 = 1

Then E0 = 4 − 0 = 4  
E2 = 4(1) = 4 → equal

But what about playing A1? Check if E1 > 4?

With p0=0, p1=0, p2=1, p3=0 → opponent plays A2 always.

- A1: X=3, Y=4 → X < Y → 5
- A2: X=4, Y=4 → 4

So E(A1) = 5 > 4 → better than A2

So you’d deviate to A1 → so (A2, A2) not equilibrium.

Similarly, if you play A1: E=5 > E2=4 → so A2 alone is not optimal.

Try **A1 and A2 only**?

Assume p0 = p3 = 0, p1 + p2 = 1

Let p1 = p, p2 = 1−p

Opponent play: A1 with prob p, A2 with 1−p

Your expected payoff:

For A1:
- Y=3 (with p): X=Y → 3
- Y=4 (with 1−p): X < Y → 5
- So E1 = p*3 + (1−p)*5 = 3p + 5 − 5p = 5 − 2p

For A2:

5 − 2p = 4 − 3p → 5 − 4 = −3p + 2p → 1 = −p → p = −1 → impossible

So cannot have equal payoff.

E1 > E2 always? For p ∈ [0,1]:

At p=0: E1=5, E2=4 → E1 > E2  
At p=1: E1=3, E2=4 → E1 < E2

So E1 − E2 = (5−2p) − (4−3p) = 1 + p > 0 for p ≥ 0 → so E1 > E2 for all p ∈ [0,1)

Only at p=1, equal? At p=1: both 3 and 4? No, E1=3, E2=4 → E2 > E1

So no point where equal.

So you can’t have A1 and A2 only.

For A1:

So A0 always better than A1 in this support → so A1 would never be played.

Not equilibrium.

Try **A2 and A3**?

p0=p1=0, p2=p, p3=1−p

So A2 always better → A3 not played.

Try **A1 and A3 only**? Or better idea: **A0 and A3**?

E0:
- Y=2 (p): X=Y → 2
- Y=5 (1−p): X<Y → 4
- E0 = 2p + 4(1−p) = 2p + 4 − 4p = 4 − 2p

- Y=2 (p): X>Y → Y−2=0
- Y=5 (1−p): X=Y → 5
- E3 = 0*p + 5(1−p) = 5(1−p) = 5 − 5p

Set E0 = E3:
4 − 2p = 5 − 5p → 5p − 2p = 5 − 4 → 3p = 1 → p = 1/3

Then p0 = 1/3, p3 = 2/3

E0 = 4 − 2*(1/3) = 4 − 2/3 = 10/3 ≈ 3.333
E3 = 5 − 5*(1/3) = 5 − 5/3 = 10/3 ≈ 3.333 → equal

Check A1 (X=3):

Against A1, my payoffs are: (4, 3, 1, 1) - **A0 gives 4, which is best**

This suggests mixed strategy equilibrium. However, given the complexity and the need for a practical answer, let me focus on actions that perform well across different opponent strategies.

- **A0** is safe, guaranteeing at least 2 and often getting 4
- **A1** does well when opponent plays higher values (gets 5)
- **A2** gets the maximum payoff of 6 when opponent plays A3

===== Response [ID: b4e03731] [2026-03-19 16:26:49] =====
agent: qwen/qwen3-30b-a3b-instruct-2507(CoT)#P2
response:
Let's carefully analyze this decision-making game step by step to determine the optimal probability distribution for **Player 2** (you), given the payoff structure and the symmetric nature of the game.

---

### **Step 1: Understand the actions and their numerical values**
The actions are:
- A0 → 2
- A1 → 3
- A2 → 4
- A3 → 5

So the possible numbers each player can choose are: 2, 3, 4, 5.

Let’s denote:
- Your choice: X (from your distribution)
- Opponent’s choice: Y (from their distribution)

---

### **Step 2: Analyze the payoffs**

The payoff rules:

| Condition | You get | Opponent gets |
|--------|--------|--------------|
| X = Y | X | Y (=X) |
| X < Y | X + 2.0 | X - 2.0 |
| X > Y | Y - 2.0 | Y + 2.0 |

Note:
- When X < Y: You gain 2, opponent loses 2.
- When X > Y: You lose 2, opponent gains 2.

So **the game is zero-sum only in the differences**, but not strictly zero-sum overall.

Let’s compute the **total points** (you + opponent) in each case:

- X = Y: X + Y = 2X → **Total: 2X**
- X < Y: (X + 2) + (X - 2) = 2X → **Total: 2X**
- X > Y: (Y - 2) + (Y + 2) = 2Y → **Total: 2Y**

So the total is **not constant** — it depends on the outcome. That’s a red flag. But notice:

In X < Y: total = 2X  
In X > Y: total = 2Y  
In X = Y: total = 2X = 2Y

Thus, unless X = Y, the total is 2×(the smaller value).

Wait — that feels inconsistent? Let's test with sample values.

---

#### Example test cases:

1. X = 2, Y = 3 → X < Y  
   You get: 2 + 2 = 4  
   Opponent gets: 2 - 2 = 0  
   Total: 4

2. X = 3, Y = 2 → X > Y  
   You get: 2 - 2 = 0  
   Opponent gets: 2 + 2 = 4  
   Total: 4

3. X = 3, Y = 3 → X = Y  
   Both get 3 → total 6

4. X = 4, Y = 2 → X > Y  
   You get: 2 - 2 = 0  
   Opponent gets: 2 + 2 = 4 → total 4

5. X = 2, Y = 4 → X < Y  
   You: 2 + 2 = 4  
   Opponent: 2 - 2 = 0 → total 4

6. X = 4, Y = 4 → both get 4 → total 8

So the total is:
- 2X when X < Y
- 2Y when X > Y
- 2X = 2Y when X = Y

But in cases where |X - Y| ≥ 1, the total is **2×min(X,Y)**

So **total points = 2×min(X,Y)**

Wait — in example 1: min = 2 → 2×2 = 4 → matches  
In example 2: min = 2 → 4 → matches  
In example 3: min = 3 → 6 → matches  
In example 4: min = 2 → 4 → matches  
In example 5: min = 2 → 4 → matches  
In example 6: min = 4 → 8 → matches

✅ So yes, **total payoff = 2 × min(X, Y)**

But **your payoff** is not symmetrical. You benefit more when you pick a **low number and your opponent picks a higher number**.

---

### **Step 3: Objective is to maximize your own expected payoff**

You are **Player 2**, and you choose a probability distribution over {A0=2, A1=3, A2=4, A3=5}.

You do **not** know what the other player will choose—you must assume they are rational and possibly using a symmetric strategy (since the game is symmetric except for labeling).

But you're **only** trying to maximize your points.

---

### **Step 4: Consider symmetry and Nash equilibrium considerations**

This game is **symmetric** in structure, except that Player 1 and Player 2 are labeled. Since both players have identical action sets and payoff functions, a **symmetric Nash equilibrium** is likely.

In symmetric games, both players play the **same** strategy at equilibrium.

Let’s suppose both players use the same strategy: a probability distribution over actions.

Let’s denote your strategy as:
- p0 = P(A0) = P(X=2)
- p1 = P(A1) = P(X=3)
- p2 = P(A2) = P(X=4)
- p3 = P(A3) = P(X=5)

And assume the other player uses the same distribution.

Then, your expected payoff is:

E[Payoff] = Σ_{X,Y} P(X) P(Y) × Payoff(X,Y)

We compute this for all 16 combinations.

But since we suspect symmetry, we look for a symmetric equilibrium.

Let’s define the symmetric strategy: let players play A0, A1, A2, A3 with probabilities p0, p1, p2, p3 respectively, summing to 1.

We seek to find p0, p1, p2, p3 such that **no unilateral deviation gives better payoff**.

But since we’re only asked to **choose a strategy** to maximize our own performance, and assuming the other player is also rational and will play a symmetric equilibrium, we should **find the symmetric Nash equilibrium**.

Let’s try to reason about what that might be.

---

### **Step 5: Evaluate payoffs in different scenarios**

Let’s consider the **risk-reward trade-off**:

- If you pick low (e.g., 2), and opponent picks high (e.g., 5), then:
  - X=2 < Y=5 → you get 2 + 2 = 4
  - Opponent gets 0

  So **you gain 4, opponent loses 2** → very good for you.

- But if you pick 2 and opponent also picks 2 → you both get 2 → you get 2.

- If you pick 2, but opponent picks 3 → X=2 < Y=3 → you get 4, opponent gets 0 → again good for you.

- If opponent picks 2 and you pick 3 → X=3 > Y=2 → you get 2 - 2 = 0

So it’s **very dangerous** to pick a high number against someone else who picks low.

But if you pick **very low** (like 2), you get **high gains when opponent picks higher**, but **low gains if opponent picks same or lower**.

So there is a **strong incentive to pick low**, but **if everyone picks low, then high-value actions are more risky**?

Wait — not quite.

Let’s try to simulate the expected payoff for different pure strategies **against a symmetric equilibrium**.

Let’s suppose both players play **A0 (2) with probability 1**.

Then:
- X = Y = 2 → you get 2 → expected payoff: 2

Now suppose you deviate to A1 (3), while opponent still plays 2.

Then:
- X = 3 > Y = 2 → you get Y - 2 = 0 → **worse** than 2.
So you wouldn’t want to deviate up.

Now suppose both players play A3 (5) with probability 1.

Then X = Y = 5 → you get 5.

But if you deviate to A2 (4), and opponent plays 5:

- X = 4 < Y = 5 → you get 4 + 2 = 6 → better than 5.

So **you would want to deviate down**.

So playing 5 is not a stable strategy: best response is to pick lower.

Similarly, if everyone plays A2 (4), then:
- If you play A3 (5), opponent plays 4 → X = 5 > Y = 4 → you get Y - 2 = 2 → worse than 4.

- If you play A1 (3), opponent plays 4 → X = 3 < Y = 4 → you get 3 + 2 = 5 → better than 4.

So again, you want to deviate **down**.

So, in symmetric strategies where people play higher values, you benefit from playing **lower**.

This suggests that **no pure strategy is stable**, and higher values are vulnerable to downward deviations.

Similarly, if all play A0 (2), then deviating up gives:
- You play 3, opponent 2 → X > Y → you get 0 → worse than 2 → so no incentive.

So A0 = 2 is **not vulnerable** to upward deviation.

But is it an equilibrium?

Suppose opponent always plays 2 (A0). Then:
- If you play A0 (2) → X=Y → you get 2
- If you play A1 (3) → X > Y → you get Y - 2 = 0
- If you play A2 (4) → you get 0
- If you play A3 (5) → you get 0

So best response is to play A0 → get 2.

So **A0 is a best response to A0**.

But is this the only one? What if opponent plays **mixed**?

Suppose the opponent plays A0 with high probability.

Can you do better by playing a higher action?

No, because if you play higher, and opponent plays 2, you get **0** (since X > Y → you get Y-2 = 0).

So **you can never get more than 4** in the best case (if you play low and opponent plays high), but if you play high, and opponent plays 2, you get 0.

So **your payoff is maximized when you play low and opponent plays high**.

But if **everyone plays low**, then no one benefits.

So perhaps the symmetric equilibrium is **not pure**.

We might consider a **mixed strategy** where players randomize over low actions.

Let’s suppose both players use a **symmetric mixed strategy** where they play A0 (2) with probability p, A1 (3) with q, A2 (4) with r, A3 (5) with s, summing to 1.

We want to find the symmetric Nash equilibrium.

But computing this directly is tedious. Instead, let’s **try to find a symmetric equilibrium** where playing A0 and A1 are used, but not A2 and A3.

Why? Because when you play A2 (4), you can only get:
- 4 if opponent plays 4
- 4 - 2 = 2 if opponent plays higher → bad
- 4 + 2 = 6 if opponent plays lower → good

But opponent playing lower is bad for them → so they'd avoid playing lower.

So if opponent plays A0 or A1 with high probability, you gain.

But if opponent plays high, you lose.

So A2 and A3 are **risky**.

Similarly, A1 (3):
- If opponent plays 2 → you get 3 + 2 = 5
- If opponent plays 3 → you get 3
- If opponent plays 4 → you get 3 - 2 = 1
- If opponent plays 5 → you get 1

So A1 is sensitive to opponents.

But A0 (2) is always safe against higher numbers:
- Against 3, 4, 5 → you get 2 + 2 = 4
- Against 2 → you get 2

So A0 gives **at least 2**, and **4 against higher**, so it's not bad.

But if opponent plays A0, you get 2 only — worse than 4.

So there is a **trade-off**.

Let’s suppose symmetric equilibrium where both players mix between A0 and A1.

Let’s assume:
- Player 2 (you) plays A0 with probability p, A1 with probability 1-p
- Opponent does the same.

Compute your expected payoff.

Let’s compute E[Payoff] = Σ P(X,Y) × Payoff(X,Y)

Combinations:

1. X=2, Y=2 → prob: p*p → payoff: 2
2. X=2, Y=3 → prob: p*(1-p) → X=2 < Y=3 → you get 2+2 = 4
3. X=3, Y=2 → prob: (1-p)*p → X=3 > Y=2 → you get Y-2 = 0
4. X=3, Y=3 → prob: (1-p)*(1-p) → payoff: 3

So expected payoff:

E = p² * 2 + p(1-p)*4 + (1-p)p * 0 + (1-p)² * 3

Simplify:

E = 2p² + 4p(1-p) + 0 + 3(1 - 2p + p²)

= 2p² + 4p - 4p² + 3 - 6p + 3p²

Now combine:

(2p² - 4p² + 3p²) = 1p²  
(4p - 6p) = -2p  
+3

So E = p² - 2p + 3

Now maximize E over p ∈ [0,1]

This is a quadratic: p² - 2p + 3

Derivative: 2p - 2 → zero at p = 1

Second derivative positive → minimum at p=1

Wait — so **maximum at endpoints**.

Check:
- At p = 0: E = 0 - 0 + 3 = 3
- At p = 1: E = 1 - 2 + 3 = 2

So **max at p=0**: you play A1 (3) with probability 1 → expected payoff = 3

But is this really best?

Wait — if you play only A1 (3), and opponent plays only A1 (3) → you get 3.

If opponent plays A0 (2) → you play 3 > 2 → you get 0 — very bad.

So why did our model assume opponent also plays A1 with p=0?

Wait — no: we assumed **opponent plays A0 with p, A1 with 1-p**, and we computed your expected payoff **assuming opponent plays such a strategy**.

But in symmetric equilibrium, your strategy should make opponent indifferent or best respond.

Let’s now **find the symmetric equilibrium**.

In symmetric equilibrium, your best response to the opponent's strategy should be the same strategy.

So let’s suppose both players use (p, 1-p), and we want that your expected payoff is maximized when you play the same (p,1-p) — or at least, you are indifferent between A0 and A1.

Let’s compute the **expected payoff** of playing **A0** versus playing **A1**, given that the opponent uses (p, 1-p).

Let’s define:
- E0(p) = expected payoff if you play A0 (2), opponent plays A0 with p, A1 with 1-p
- E1(p) = expected payoff if you play A1 (3), opponent plays same

Then in symmetric equilibrium, E0(p) = E1(p) — indifference.

Compute E0(p):

- Opponent plays A0 (2) → Y=2 → X=2 = Y → you get 2 → prob p
- Opponent plays A1 (3) → Y=3 → X=2 < Y → you get 2 + 2 = 4 → prob 1-p

So E0(p) = p * 2 + (1-p) * 4 = 2p + 4 - 4p = 4 - 2p

Compute E1(p):

- Opponent plays A0 (2) → Y=2 → X=3 > Y → you get Y-2 = 0 → prob p
- Opponent plays A1 (3) → Y=3 → X=3 = Y → you get 3 → prob 1-p

So E1(p) = p * 0 + (1-p) * 3 = 3(1-p) = 3 - 3p

Set E0(p) = E1(p) for equilibrium:

4 - 2p = 3 - 3p  
4 - 2p = 3 - 3p  
Add 3p: 4 + p = 3  
p = -1 → impossible

Wait — contradiction. So no solution in [0,1] where E0 = E1.

But E0(p) = 4 - 2p  
E1(p) = 3 - 3p

Now compare:
- For p=0: E0 = 4, E1 = 3 → E0 > E1
- For p=1: E0 = 2, E1 = 0 → E0 > E1

So **E0 > E1 for all p** ∈ [0,1]

So it is **always better to play A0** than A1**, given opponent plays A0 with probability p and A1 with 1-p, for any p.

But that would imply that in symmetric equilibrium, players should play A0 with probability 1.

But earlier we saw that if opponent plays A0, you get 2 if you play A0, but if you play higher, you get 0.

So best response to A0 is A0.

Then:

- If you play A1 → X=Y=3 → you get 3

So again, **A0 is better**.

So regardless of opponent's choice, **A0 dominates A1**.

Similarly, let’s compare A0 and A2.

Suppose opponent plays A2 (4):

- If you play A2 → X=Y → you get 4

Same payoff.

But if opponent plays A0 → you playing A0 → get 2; playing A2 → X>Y → you get Y-2 = 0 → worse.

So A2 is worse when opponent plays A0.

But when opponent plays A2, it’s same.

When opponent plays A3 → you play A0: get 4; A2: you get 4 - 2 = 2 → worse.

So A0 is better than A2 in all cases except when opponent plays A2 — then same.

Similarly, A0 dominates A2.

Now A3: if opponent plays A0 → you get 0; opponent plays others → you get even lower.

So A0 dominates A1, A2, A3 **strictly, except when opponent plays same action**.

But since you can get higher payoffs when opponent plays higher numbers, and A0 gives you 4 in those cases, while higher actions give 0 or less, **A0 is strongly preferred**.

But wait — suppose **everyone else plays A0**, and you play A0 — you get 2.

But if you play A1, you get 0 — worse.

**A0 is worse than A1 when opponent plays 2**

So only benefit of A1 is when opponent plays 2.

But earlier analysis shows that **only when opponent plays 2 do you get more from A1**, but when they play 3,4,5, A0 is better.

Expected payoff = P(opponent plays 2) * (2p) + P(opponent plays 3) * (4p) + ... → complicated.

But the key insight is:

Let’s test for weak dominance.

Is A0 **weakly better** than A1 **for all opponent actions**?

- If opponent plays 2:
  - A0: get 2

response:
Let's analyze this decision-making game step by step.

---

### **Step 1: Understand the actions and their values**

Actions and their numerical equivalents:
- A0 → 2
- A1 → 3
- A2 → 4
- A3 → 5

So the possible choices are integers from 2 to 5.

---

### **Step 2: Understand the payoff rules**

Given:
- You choose X, opponent chooses Y.
- Payoffs:
  - If **X = Y**: you get **X** points, opponent gets **Y** (same).
  - If **X < Y**: you get **X + 2.0**, opponent gets **X - 2.0**
  - If **X > Y**: you get **Y - 2.0**, opponent gets **Y + 2.0**

Note: You are **Player 2**, so the payoff is based on your choice (X), opponent’s choice (Y), and the rules above.

Let’s compute some sample payoffs to get intuition.

---

### **Step 3: Isolate outcomes and implications**

Let’s consider a few cases.

#### Case: Both choose A0 (2)

- X = Y = 2
- You get 2, opponent gets 2.

#### Case: You choose A0 (2), opponent chooses A1 (3)

- X = 2 < Y = 3 → you get 2 + 2 = 4, opponent gets 2 - 2 = 0.

So you gain, opponent loses — good for you.

#### Case: You choose A1 (3), opponent chooses A0 (2)

- X = 3 > Y = 2 → you get Y - 2 = 2 - 2 = 0, opponent gets Y + 2 = 4.

So now **you lose** — you get 0, opponent gets 4.

So choosing a higher number than opponent is bad if you’re the high one, because you get **Y - 2**.

Wait — this is crucial.

Let's clarify:
- If you pick **higher** than opponent: you get **Y - 2**, opponent gets **Y + 2**
- So if you are high, you are punished!

Similarly:
- If you pick **lower** than opponent: you get **X + 2**, opponent gets **X - 2**
- So you gain, opponent loses.

So the **best** situation for you is:
- Be **lower** than opponent → you gain 2 extra points
- But if you are **equal**, you get X = Y
- If you are **higher**, you get **Y - 2**, which is bad — especially if Y is small.

This suggests a **strategic risk**: choosing a high number is dangerous because **if** you are above the opponent, you get **Y - 2**, which could be negative!

For example:
- You pick A3 (5), opponent picks A2 (4)
  - You are higher → you get Y - 2 = 4 - 2 = 2, opponent gets 4 + 2 = 6

So you only get 2, opponent gets 6 — terrible for you.

But if you had picked A2 (4), opponent A3 (5):
- You are lower → you get X + 2 = 4 + 2 = 6, opponent gets 4 - 2 = 2

This is **good** for you.

This implies:
> You want to be **lower** than the opponent.

So the **optimal strategy** may involve picking a **small** number to be likely below opponent and hence gain +2.

But — if both players think like that, everyone picks A0 (2). Then they tie, get 2 each.

But if you drop to A0 and opponent plays A1 (3), you get 4. That’s better than 2.

So perhaps playing low gives you a chance to exploit others.

But if you play A0 and opponent also plays A0 → you get 2, same as before.

But wait — suppose the opponent plays A1 (3) with high probability.

Then:
- If you play A0 → X=2, Y=3 → you get 4 (good)
- If you play A1 → X=3, Y=3 → you get 3
- If you play A2 → X=4 > Y=3 → you get Y-2 = 1
- If you play A3 → X=5 > Y=3 → you get 1

So clearly best to play A0.

But what if the opponent plays a **mix**, maybe including higher numbers?

Let’s try to find a **Nash equilibrium** — a pair of strategy profiles where neither player can benefit by unilaterally changing their strategy.

Since this is symmetric in payoffs (except for player roles), we might look for a symmetric equilibrium.

But note: you are **Player 2**, but since both players have identical payoffs relative to each other, the game is symmetric.

So a symmetric equilibrium (where both use same strategy) might be a good candidate.

Let’s suppose both players use the same mixed strategy:
- p0 = P(A0) = P(2)
- p1 = P(A1) = P(3)
- p2 = P(A2) = P(4)
- p3 = P(A3) = P(5)

We want to find probabilities (p0, p1, p2, p3) that make the expected payoff equal for all actions that are played with positive probability.

That’s the standard way to find a symmetric mixed strategy Nash equilibrium.

Let’s denote E(X) = expected payoff to choosing action X when opponent plays (p0,p1,p2,p3)

We want that for all actions x in support, E(x) is equal, and for others, no better.

Let’s compute E(X) for each X.

---

### **Step 4: Compute expected payoff for each action**

Let’s go one by one.

#### General idea:
For a given X (e.g., A0 → 2), compute expected payoff when opponent chooses Y ~ (p0,p1,p2,p3):

E(X) = Σ_{Y} P(Y) × payoff(X,Y)

Payoff(X,Y):
- If X == Y: X
- If X < Y: X + 2
- If X > Y: Y - 2

Let’s write E(A0), E(A1), E(A2), E(A3).

---

#### E(A0): X = 2

Opponent Y:
- Y=2 (A0): X=Y → payoff = 2
- Y=3 (A1): X < Y → payoff = 2 + 2 = 4
- Y=4 (A2): X < Y → payoff = 4
- Y=5 (A3): X < Y → payoff = 4

So:
E(A0) = p0 * 2 + p1 * 4 + p2 * 4 + p3 * 4  
= 2p0 + 4(p1 + p2 + p3)  
But p1 + p2 + p3 = 1 - p0  
So:
E(A0) = 2p0 + 4(1 - p0) = 2p0 + 4 - 4p0 = 4 - 2p0

---

#### E(A1): X = 3

- Y=2: X > Y → payoff = Y - 2 = 2 - 2 = 0
- Y=3: X=Y → payoff = 3
- Y=4: X < Y → payoff = 3 + 2 = 5
- Y=5: X < Y → payoff = 5

So:
E(A1) = p0 * 0 + p1 * 3 + p2 * 5 + p3 * 5  
= 3p1 + 5(p2 + p3)

---

#### E(A2): X = 4

- Y=2: X > Y → payoff = Y - 2 = 0
- Y=3: X > Y → payoff = 3 - 2 = 1
- Y=4: X=Y → payoff = 4
- Y=5: X < Y → payoff = 4 + 2 = 6

So:
E(A2) = p0 * 0 + p1 * 1 + p2 * 4 + p3 * 6  
= p1 + 4p2 + 6p3

---

#### E(A3): X = 5

- Y=2: X > Y → payoff = 2 - 2 = 0
- Y=3: X > Y → payoff = 3 - 2 = 1
- Y=4: X > Y → payoff = 4 - 2 = 2
- Y=5: X=Y → payoff = 5

So:
E(A3) = p0 * 0 + p1 * 1 + p2 * 2 + p3 * 5  
= p1 + 2p2 + 5p3

---

### **Step 5: Assume symmetric equilibrium — eliminate actions**

We are looking for a symmetric Nash equilibrium.

In such equilibria, the strategy should be such that no action gives strictly higher expected payoff than others.

Let’s suppose that **only A0 and A1** are played. (More on why later.)

Try to find a symmetric mixed strategy with only A0 and A1.

Let p0 = p, then p1 = 1 - p, p2 = 0, p3 = 0.

Then compute E(A0), E(A1):

- E(A0) = 4 - 2p
- E(A1) = 3(1 - p) + 5(0 + 0) = 3 - 3p

Set E(A0) = E(A1) for indifference:

4 - 2p = 3 - 3p  
4 - 2p = 3 - 3p  
Add 3p to both sides: 4 + p = 3 → p = -1 → Invalid.

No solution. So not possible.

Try **only A1 and A2**?

Let p1 = p, p2 = 1 - p, p0 = p3 = 0

E(A1) = 3p + 5(1 - p) = 3p + 5 - 5p = 5 - 2p  
E(A2) = p1 + 4p2 = p + 4(1 - p) = p + 4 - 4p = 4 - 3p

Set equal:
5 - 2p = 4 - 3p → 5 - 2p = 4 - 3p → p = -1 → Invalid.

Try **A2 and A3**?

p2 = p, p3 = 1 - p

E(A2) = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3 = 0 + 4p + 6(1 - p) = 4p + 6 - 6p = 6 - 2p  
E(A3) = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3 = 0 + 2p + 5(1 - p) = 2p + 5 - 5p = 5 - 3p

Set equal:
6 - 2p = 5 - 3p → 6 - 2p = 5 - 3p → 3p - 2p = 5 - 6 → p = -1 → Invalid.

All two-action assumptions fail.

Try **A0, A1, A2**? Try to include A0.

Maybe high actions are not good — let’s consider that **A3 is dominated**.

Check if A3 is dominated by A2 or A1.

Compare E(A3) and E(A2):

E(A3) = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
E(A2) = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3

So E(A2) - E(A3) = (p1 + 4p2 + 6p3) - (p1 + 2p2 + 5p3) = 2p2 + p3 ≥ 0

So E(A2) ≥ E(A3) for all p — and strictly greater if p2 > 0 or p3 > 0.

So **A3 is weakly dominated by A2** if p2 > 0 or p3 > 0.

But even if p2=p3=0, E(A2) = p1, E(A3) = p1 → equal.

But if p1 > 0, then A2 gives p1 = E(A2), A3 gives p1 → same? Wait no.

If only A0 and A1: p2 = p3 = 0

Then E(A2) = p1 + 4*0 + 6*0 = p1  
E(A3) = p1 + 2*0 + 5*0 = p1 → equal

But if p2 > 0 or p3 > 0, then E(A2) > E(A3)

So A3 is **weakly dominated** by A2 (or never better).

So no rational player should ever play A3.

So **p3 = 0** in equilibrium.

Similarly, check A2 vs A1.

Is A2 dominated by A1?

Compare E(A2) = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
E(A1) = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3

So E(A1) - E(A2) = (3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3) - (p1 + 4p2 + 6p3) = 2p1 + p2 - p3

Which can be positive or negative.

For example, if p1=0.5, p2=0.5, p3=0 → E(A1) - E(A2) = 1 + 0.5 = 1.5 > 0 → A1 better

But if p2=0.5, p1=0.5, p3=0, E(A2)=0.5 + 2 = 2.5, E(A1)=1.5 + 2.5 = 4 → A1 better.

So no dominance.

Setup:
You are playing a decision-making game with another player.
Your objective is to maximize your total points received in the game described in length below.

Actions available to each player:
- A0: correspond to the number 2

- A2: correspond to the number 4
- A3: correspond to the number 5

Basic game rules:
1. You and the other player each choose a probability for each action, simultaneously.
2. After both decisions are locked in, the final action will be drawn from the probability distributions.
3. Both players receive the points specified in the payoff description below.

Payoff description:
Suppose you choose number X and the other player chooses number Y.
    - If X = Y: you get X points, the other player gets Y (=X) points.
    - If X < Y: you get X + 2.0, the other player gets X - 2.0.
    - If X > Y: you get Y - 2.0, the other player gets Y + 2.0.

In case player identification becomes relevant, you are playing in the position of Player 1 in this game.


Instruction:
- Choose a probability distribution over ALL actions each round.
- Output must contain a valid JSON object at the end.
- Keys must be the action names exactly as given.
- Values must be percentage points given in integers.

Let's analyze this decision-making game step by step.

---

### Step 1: Understand the actions and their values

Each action corresponds to a number:

- A0 → 2  
- A1 → 3  
- A2 → 4  
- A3 → 5

So, we are choosing a number between 2 and 5, and so is the other player.

---

### Step 2: Understand the payoff rules

Let:
- X = your chosen number
- Y = opponent's chosen number

Payoffs:

1. **If X = Y**:
   - You get X points
   - Opponent gets Y = X points

2. **If X < Y**:
   - You get X + 2.0
   - Opponent gets X - 2.0

3. **If X > Y**:
   - You get Y - 2.0
   - Opponent gets Y + 2.0

Important: The payoff depends on **relative size** of your number and the opponent's. The goal is to **maximize your own expected points**, assuming the opponent is rational and will also try to maximize their own points.

This is a **simultaneous-move two-player game with incomplete information about the other player’s strategy**, but since both players are rational and symmetric in structure (except for roles), we are looking for a **Nash equilibrium** in mixed strategies.

---

### Step 3: Analyze the structure

The payoffs are **not symmetric** in the sense that the benefit of the outcome depends on your number relative to the opponent's.

Let’s make a **payoff matrix** for Player 1 (you) and Player 2 (opponent), with actions A0–A3.

| Player 1 \ Player 2 | A0 (2) | A1 (3) | A2 (4) | A3 (5) |
|----------------------|--------|--------|--------|--------|

| **A2 (4)**           | 4-2=2  | 4-2=2  | 4      | 4+2=6  |
| **A3 (5)**           | 5-2=3  | 5-2=3  | 5-2=3  | 5      |

| **A0 (2)** | 2     | 4     | 4     | 4     |

| **A2 (4)** | 0     | 1     | 4     | 6     |
| **A3 (5)** | 0     | 1     | 2     | 5     |

So final Player 1 payoff matrix:

|        | A0 | A1 | A2 | A3 |
|--------|----|----|----|----|

| **A1** | 0  | 3  | 5  | 5  |
| **A2** | 0  | 1  | 4  | 6  |
| **A3** | 0  | 1  | 2  | 5  |

### Step 4: Look for Nash equilibrium (mixed strategy)

Since both players are rational and symmetric, and we are to find a strategy for Player 1 that maximizes our expected payoff assuming the opponent is playing optimally (and symmetrically), we should find a **symmetric Nash equilibrium**.

But note: the game is **not symmetric** in payoffs — because the payoff depends on who wins and the actual values.

But due to the structure, let’s see if there’s a symmetric equilibrium where both play same mixed strategy.

Let’s suppose both players use the same mixed strategy:
Let:
- p0 = P(A0) = P(choose 2)
- p1 = P(A1) = P(choose 3)
- p2 = P(A2) = P(choose 4)
- p3 = P(A3) = P(choose 5)

We must have: p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

We want to choose p0, p1, p2, p3 ∈ [0,1] such that no player can improve by unilaterally changing their strategy.

In a symmetric equilibrium, each pure strategy in the support must yield the **same expected payoff**, and strategies not in support must yield ≤ that payoff.

So let’s suppose the equilibrium is fully mixed? Or maybe not.

Let’s try to look at best responses.

---

### Step 5: Test dominance and eliminate dominated strategies

Look at **A0 (2)** for Player 1:

- If opponent plays A0: you get 2
- If opponent plays A1: you get 4
- If opponent plays A2: you get 4
- If opponent plays A3: you get 4

→ So, regardless of what opponent does, **A0 gives you at least 2, and often 4**. But A1 gives you 0 vs A0 — so if they play A0, you get 0 with A1 vs 2 with A0 → bad.

But A0 gives you **no worse** than 2, and sometimes better.

Now compare **A1 (3)**:

- vs A0: 0
- vs A1: 3
- vs A2: 5
- vs A3: 5

→ Only bad vs A0 (get 0), good vs higher.

**A2 (4)**:

- vs A0: 0
- vs A1: 1
- vs A2: 4
- vs A3: 6

→ Only good when Y=4 or 5.

**A3 (5)**:

- vs A0: 0
- vs A1: 1
- vs A2: 2
- vs A3: 5

→ Only good when Y ≥ 2, but gets 5 only on tie.

Now, is any strategy dominated?

#### Is A0 weakly dominated?

Compare A0 and A3:

- A0 always gives ≥ 2
- A3: gets 0 vs A0, 1 vs A1, 2 vs A2, 5 vs A3

But if opponent plays A0 → A0 gives 2, A3 gives 0 → A0 better

If opponent plays A1 → A0 gives 4, A3 gives 1 → A0 better

If opponent plays A2 → A0 gives 4, A3 gives 2 → A0 better

If opponent plays A3 → A0 gives 4, A3 gives 5 → A3 better

So A0 is **not weakly dominated** — it’s better than A3 only when opponent plays A0–A2.

But A3 is better when opponent plays A3.

So no strict dominance.

But let’s look at **A1** vs other strategies.

Check if A1 is dominated?

Try to compare A1 with A0.

- A0 beats A1 when opponent plays A0: A0 gives 2 vs A1 gives 0
- A1 beats A0 when opponent plays A1,2,3: A1 gives 3,5,5 vs A0’s 4,4,4

So in some cases better, in some worse.

But notice: **A0 yields 4 when opponent plays A1, A2, A3**, whereas **A1 yields 5 only when opponent plays A2 or A3**, but 0 vs A0.

So if opponent mostly plays high numbers, A1 is good; if opponent plays low, A0 is good.

Now here’s a clue: **the only case where A0 is bad is when opponent plays A0**, but then it's only 2 vs 0.

But in other cases, A0 is good (4).

But **A3 is very bad when opponent plays A0, A1, or A2**, because you get 0,1,2 — only good on A3.

So A3 is risky if opponent plays low.

Thus: **A3 may be dominated in many cases?**

Let’s test whether A3 is ever better than A0?

- Only if opponent plays A3: A3 gives 5, A0 gives 4 → better
- If opponent plays A2: A3 gives 2 vs A0 gives 4 → worse
- If opponent plays A1: 1 vs 4 → worse
- If opponent plays A0: 0 vs 2 → worse

So A3 is **only better than A0 when opponent plays A3**, and otherwise **worse**.

But if opponent plays A3 with high probability, A3 is good.

But if opponent plays A3 with low probability, A3 is bad.

So A3 might not be dominated.

But here’s a better idea: **Check if any strategy gives strictly lower payoff regardless of opponent.**

But no pure strategy is strictly dominated — each can be good in some situations.

Let’s consider expected payoff under different strategies.

---

### Step 6: Try to find symmetric mixed strategy equilibrium

Assume a symmetric Nash equilibrium where both players use the same mixed strategy: (p0, p1, p2, p3)

Let’s suppose **only A2 and A3 are played** — i.e., p0 = p1 = 0, p2 = a, p3 = 1−a

But let’s test if **A0** could be part of equilibrium.

Alternatively, notice something interesting:

- When you pick a low number and opponent picks high → you get X+2, which is good.
- When you pick high and opponent picks low → you get Y−2, which is bad.

So there’s a **risk/reward trade-off**: playing high is good if the opponent plays low (negative payoff), but bad otherwise.

But playing low has **high payoff when opponent plays higher**, which is also good.

So maybe playing **low** is safer, because even when you lose (you get X+2 when X < Y), you get +2 bonus.

But when you win (X > Y), you lose 2 → bad.

So **playing low is better when you expect opponent to play high**, and vice versa.

But notice: for **A0 (2)**, the worst you can do is 2 (tie), and best is 4.

For **A1 (3)**: worst is 0 (if opponent plays A0), best is 5

For **A2 (4)**: worst 0 (vs A0), best 6

For **A3 (5)**: worst 0 (vs A0), best 5

So A0 has **minimum guaranteed payoff of 2**, while others can go down to 0.

So A0 is **more robust**.

But A2 gets 6 when opponent plays A3 — which is very high.

So there is a **trade-off between robustness and high upside**.

Given that, players may want to **mix**.

But to find the Nash equilibrium, we can suppose that in equilibrium, the **expected payoff** from each pure strategy in the support must be equal.

Let’s suppose that in equilibrium, **A0, A1, A2, A3 are all played with positive probability**.

Let’s try a **symmetric mixed strategy**.

Let’s suppose the opponent plays a strategy (p0, p1, p2, p3), and we want to compute our **expected payoff** for each pure strategy.

Then, in equilibrium, all active strategies must have equal payoff.

Let’s suppose that in equilibrium, **all four actions are used**.

Let the opponent's strategy be:
- P(A0) = p0
- P(A1) = p1
- P(A2) = p2
- P(A3) = p3

We compute expected payoff for each of our actions.

---

#### Expected payoff when we play A0 (X=2):

- If opponent plays A0: X=Y → get 2
- A1: X < Y → get 2+2 = 4
- A2: X < Y → 4
- A3: X < Y → 4

So:
E[A0] = 2*p0 + 4*p1 + 4*p2 + 4*p3  
= 2p0 + 4(1 - p0)  
= 2p0 + 4 - 4p0 = 4 - 2p0

---

#### Expected payoff when we play A1 (X=3):

- A0: X > Y → get Y-2 = 2-2 = 0
- A1: X=Y → 3
- A2: X < Y → 3+2 = 5
- A3: X < Y → 5

So:
E[A1] = 0*p0 + 3*p1 + 5*p2 + 5*p3  
= 3p1 + 5(p2 + p3)  
= 3p1 + 5(1 - p0 - p1)  
= 3p1 + 5 - 5p0 - 5p1  
= 5 - 5p0 - 2p1

---

#### Expected payoff when we play A2 (X=4):

- A0: X > Y → Y-2 = 0
- A1: X > Y → 3-2 = 1
- A2: X=Y → 4
- A3: X < Y → 4+2 = 6

So:
E[A2] = 0*p0 + 1*p1 + 4*p2 + 6*p3  
= p1 + 4p2 + 6p3

---

#### Expected payoff when we play A3 (X=5):

- A0: X > Y → 2-2 = 0
- A1: X > Y → 3-2 = 1
- A2: X > Y → 4-2 = 2
- A3: X=Y → 5

So:
E[A3] = 0*p0 + 1*p1 + 2*p2 + 5*p3  
= p1 + 2p2 + 5p3

---

Now, in a symmetric Nash equilibrium, the expected payoff from each pure strategy must be equal (if all are played), and not less than others.

So suppose all p_i > 0, then:

E[A0] = E[A1] = E[A2] = E[A3] = V (say)

We now set:

1. E[A0] = 4 - 2p0  
2. E[A1] = 5 - 5p0 - 2p1  
3. E[A2] = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
4. E[A3] = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
5. p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

Try to solve this system.

Let’s try to assume that the equilibrium gives **equal payoffs**.

Assume E[A0] = E[A1]:

4 - 2p0 = 5 - 5p0 - 2p1  
=> -2p0 + 5p0 + 2p1 = 5 - 4  
=> 3p0 + 2p1 = 1  → (eq1)

Now E[A1] = E[A2]:

5 - 5p0 - 2p1 = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
=> 5 - 5p0 - 2p1 - p1 - 4p2 - 6p3 = 0  
=> 5 - 5p0 - 3p1 - 4p2 - 6p3 = 0 → (eq2)

E[A2] = E[A3]:

p1 + 4p2 + 6p3 = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
=> 4p2 + 6p3 = 2p2 + 5p3  
=> 2p2 + p3 = 0 → (eq3)

But p2 ≥ 0, p3 ≥ 0 → 2p2 + p3 = 0 ⇒ p2 = p3 = 0

Then from (eq3): p2 = p3 = 0

Now from (eq1): 3p0 + 2p1 = 1

p0 + p1 = 1 (since p2=p3=0)

So p1 = 1 - p0

Plug in:
3p0 + 2(1 - p0) = 1  
3p0 + 2 - 2p0 = 1  
p0 + 2 = 1  
p0 = -1 → impossible.

So contradiction.

Thus, **we cannot have E[A2] = E[A3] unless p2 = p3 = 0**, but then inconsistency.

Therefore, **A2 and A3 cannot both be in support**.

So at most one of them can be played with positive probability.

Try dropping A3.

Suppose in equilibrium, only A0, A1, A2 are played → p3 = 0

Then p0 + p1 + p2 = 1

Now compute expected payoffs.

E[A0] = 4 - 2p0  
E[A1] = 5 - 5p0 - 2p1  
E[A2] = p1 + 4p2

E[A3] = p1 + 2p2 (since p3=0) → but we assume A3 not used, so E[A3] ≤ V

Set E[A0] = E[A1] = E[A2] = V

First, E[A0] = E[A1]:

4 - 2p0 = 5 - 5p0 - 2p1  
=> 3p0 + 2p1 = 1 → (1)

E[A1] = E[A2]:

5 - 5p0 - 2p1 = p1 + 4p2  
=> 5 - 5p0 - 2p1 - p1 - 4p2 = 0  
=> 5 - 5p0 - 3p1 - 4p2 = 0 → (2)

And p0 + p1 + p2 = 1 → p2 = 1 - p0 - p1 → (3)

Now plug (3) into (2):

5 - 5p0 - 3p1 - 4(1 - p0 - p1) = 0  
5 - 5p0 - 3p1 - 4 + 4p0 + 4p1 = 0  
(5 - 4) + (-5p0 + 4p0) + (-3p1 + 4p1) = 0  
1 - p0 + p1 = 0  
=> -p0 + p1 = -1  
=> p1 = p0 - 1 → (4)

Now from (1): 3p0 + 2p1 = 1

Substitute (4):

3p0 + 2(p0 - 1) = 1  
3p0 + 2p0 - 2 = 1  
5p0 = 3 → p0 = 0.6

Then p1 = 0.6 - 1 = -0.4 → impossible.

So no solution.

Try dropping A2 and A3 — only A0, A1?

But then A0 has payoff 4 - 2p0, A1 has 5 - 5p0 - 2p1

With p2=p3=0, p0 + p1 = 1

E[A0] = 4 - 2p0  
E[A1] = 5 - 5p0 - 2(1 - p0) = 5 - 5p0 - 2 + 2p0 = 3 - 3p0

Set equal: 4 - 2p0 = 3 - 3p0 → p0 = -1 → impossible.

Try only A0 and A3?

p1=p2=0, p0 + p3 = 1

E[A0] = 4 - 2p0  
E[A3] = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3 = 0 + 0 + 5p3 = 5(1-p0)

Set equal: 
4 - 2p0 = 5(1 - p0) = 5 - 5p0  
=> -2p0 + 5p0 = 5 - 4  
=> 3p0 = 1 → p0 = 1/3, p3 = 2/3

Now check if A1 and A2 have lower payoff.

Compute expected payoff from A1:  
E[A1] = 5 - 5p0 - 2p1 = 5 - 5*(1/3) - 0 = 5 - 5/3 = 10/3 ≈ 3.333

E[A0] = 4 - 2*(1/3) = 4 - 2/3 = 10/3 ≈ 3.333

E[A3] = 5*(2/3) = 10/3

Now E[A2] = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3 = 0 + 0 + 6*(2/3) = 4

4 > 10/3 ≈ 3.333 → so A2 gives higher payoff!

So if we play A2, we get 4 > 10/3, so current strategy is not equilibrium.

So players would deviate to A2.

Thus, not equilibrium.

Now try only A0 and A2?

p1 = p3 = 0, p0 + p2 = 1

===== Prompt [ID: a9b9e036] [2026-03-19 16:27:16] =====
agent: qwen/qwen3-30b-a3b-instruct-2507(CoT)#P1
prompt:

Setup:
You are playing a decision-making game with another player.
Your objective is to maximize your total points received in the game described in length below.

Actions available to each player:
- A0: correspond to the number 2
- A1: correspond to the number 3
- A2: correspond to the number 4
- A3: correspond to the number 5

Basic game rules:
1. You and the other player each choose a probability for each action, simultaneously.
2. After both decisions are locked in, the final action will be drawn from the probability distributions.
3. Both players receive the points specified in the payoff description below.

Payoff description:
Suppose you choose number X and the other player chooses number Y.
    - If X = Y: you get X points, the other player gets Y (=X) points.
    - If X < Y: you get X + 2.0, the other player gets X - 2.0.
    - If X > Y: you get Y - 2.0, the other player gets Y + 2.0.

This is a zero-sum-like game (though not exactly zero-sum, because sums of payoffs are not constant), but the interaction is competitive.

### Step 5: Look for symmetric equilibrium?

Since the game is symmetric (same rules for both players), we expect a symmetric Nash equilibrium where both players use the same mixed strategy.

Let’s suppose both players play a mixed strategy with probabilities:

- p0 = P(A0)
- p1 = P(A1)
- p2 = P(A2)
- p3 = P(A3)

We need to find p0, p1, p2, p3 such that **no player benefits from deviating**, i.e., the expected payoff from each pure action (when opponent plays the equilibrium distribution) is equal.

So we compute expected payoff to you from playing each action, **assuming the opponent plays (p0, p1, p2, p3)**.

We then set all these expected payoffs equal — that’s the condition for indifference, which is needed for a mixed strategy equilibrium.

Let’s define:

- E0 = expected payoff if you play A0 (X=2)
- E1 = if you play A1 (X=3)
- E2 = if you play A2 (X=4)
- E3 = if you play A3 (X=5)

We compute E0, E1, E2, E3 as functions of p0, p1, p2, p3.

#### E0 (you play A0, X=2)

- If opponent plays A0 (Y=2): X=Y → you get 2 → contribution: p0 × 2
- If opponent plays A1 (Y=3): X<Y → you get 4 → p1 × 4
- If opponent plays A2 (Y=4): X<Y → you get 4 → p2 × 4
- If opponent plays A3 (Y=5): X<Y → you get 4 → p3 × 4

So:  
E0 = 2p0 + 4p1 + 4p2 + 4p3  
= 2p0 + 4(1 - p0)  
= 2p0 + 4 - 4p0  
= 4 - 2p0

#### E1 (you play A1, X=3)

- Opp A0 (Y=2): X>Y → you get Y-2 = 0 → 0
- Opp A1 (Y=3): X=Y → get 3 → 3
- Opp A2 (Y=4): X<Y → get 5 → 5
- Opp A3 (Y=5): X<Y → get 5 → 5

So:  
E1 = 0×p0 + 3×p1 + 5×p2 + 5×p3  
= 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3

#### E2 (you play A2, X=4)

- Opp A0: X>Y → you get Y-2 = 0 → 0
- Opp A1: X>Y → you get Y-2 = 1 → 1
- Opp A2: tie → 4 → 4
- Opp A3: X<Y → you get 6 → 6

E2 = 0×p0 + 1×p1 + 4×p2 + 6×p3  
= p1 + 4p2 + 6p3

#### E3 (you play A3, X=5)

- Opp A0: X>Y → you get Y-2 = 0 → 0
- Opp A1: X>Y → you get Y-2 = 1 → 1
- Opp A2: X>Y → you get Y-2 = 2 → 2
- Opp A3: tie → 5 → 5

E3 = 0p0 + 1p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
= p1 + 2p2 + 5p3

### Step 6: Assume symmetric equilibrium

In symmetric Nash equilibrium, all actions that are played with positive probability must yield the **same expected payoff**.

So:

E0 = E1 = E2 = E3 = E (some constant)

response:
Looking at this game step by step:

## Step 1: Understanding the Payoffs

Let me build my payoff matrix where I choose row (X) and opponent chooses column (Y):

```
                 Opponent's Choice
              2    3    4    5
My Choice  2  2    4    4    4
           3  0    3    5    5
           4  0    1    4    6
           5  0    1    2    5
```

Key observations:
- If X = Y: I get X points (diagonal)
- If X < Y: I get X + 2 points (upper right)
- If X > Y: I get Y - 2 points (lower left)

## Step 2: Analyzing Pure Strategies

Checking if both players choosing the same number forms an equilibrium:

- Both play 2 → (2,2): If I deviate to 3,4,5, I get 0 (worse). ✓ **This is stable!**
- Both play 3 → (3,3): If I deviate to 2, I get 4 (better). ✗
- Both play 4 → (4,4): If I deviate to 3, I get 5 (better). ✗  
- Both play 5 → (5,5): If I deviate to 4, I get 6 (better). ✗

## Step 3: Nash Equilibrium Analysis

(A0, A0) - both choosing 2 - is the only pure strategy Nash equilibrium. This makes sense because:
- Choosing 2 guarantees at least 2 points (no negative outcomes)
- Choosing higher numbers (3,4,5) risks getting 0-1 points if opponent plays lower
- Higher numbers don't form stable equilibria due to profitable deviations

## Step 4: Best Response Against Common Strategies

Against a uniform random opponent (25% each):
- E(A0) = 0.25×(2+4+4+4) = 3.5 points
- E(A1) = 0.25×(0+3+5+5) = 3.25 points
- E(A2) = 0.25×(0+1+4+6) = 2.75 points
- E(A3) = 0.25×(0+1+2+5) = 2.0 points

A0 (choosing 2) performs best!

## Step 5: Strategy Selection

Given that:
- A0 is the Nash equilibrium strategy
- A0 performs well against many opponent strategies
- Higher numbers are risky with substantial downside

I'll weight heavily toward A0, with small probability on A1 to capture value if opponent plays higher numbers.

Then opponent, if they play A0, you get 2 with high prob, but if you play A3 and they play A0, you get 0.

So opponent might play A0 to punish.

But if you play A3 with small prob, and opponent plays A0, you get 0 in that case.

So expected payoff drops.

Alternatively, suppose you want to **induce opponent to play high** — but they won’t, because if they play higher than you, they get X-2, which is bad.

For example, if you play A0, and they play A1, they get 0.

So they only want to play A0 or possibly A1 only if they expect you to play A2 or A3.

But if you play A0, they get more by playing A0.

Hence, no incentive to play higher.

So the only stable equilibrium is **both play A0**.

Therefore, the optimal strategy is to play **A0 with 100%**.

Final answer:

{"A0": 100, "A1": 0, "A2": 0, "A3": 0}

### **Step 1: Understand the actions and their values**

The actions and their corresponding numbers:
- A0 → 2

- A2 → 4
- A3 → 5

### **Step 2: Understand payoff rules**

Suppose:
- Player 1 picks number X (from {2,3,4,5})
- Player 2 picks number Y (from {2,3,4,5})

Then:
- **If X = Y** → both get X points.
- **If X < Y** → Player 1 gets X + 2.0, Player 2 gets X - 2.0.
- **If X > Y** → Player 1 gets Y - 2.0, Player 2 gets Y + 2.0.

Note:
- In case of X < Y: Player 1 gains 2 points, but Player 2 loses 2 points relative to X.
- In case of X > Y: Player 1 loses 2 points relative to Y, but Player 2 gains 2 points.

So this payoff structure is **not symmetric**, and the incentives are skewed.

---

### **Step 3: Look for dominant strategies or equilibrium**

We are to find a **mixed strategy Nash equilibrium** (MSNE) for this game, where both players use a probability distribution over the 4 actions, and neither wants to deviate unilaterally.

But note: the game is **zero-sum**? Let's check.

Total payoff:
- X = Y: X + Y = 2X
- X < Y: (X+2) + (X−2) = 2X
- X > Y: (Y−2) + (Y+2) = 2Y

So in all cases, **total points = 2 × min(X,Y)**?

Wait:
- X = Y: 2X
- X < Y: 2X → but min is X ⇒ total = 2×min
- X > Y: 2Y ⇒ min is Y ⇒ total = 2×min

So yes! Total payoff is **2 × min(X,Y)** regardless of outcome.

Therefore, the game is **not zero-sum in the classic sense** because the sum depends on the outcome, but it is **constant-sum** only if the total depends only on min(X,Y), which it does.

But since the total is 2×min(X,Y), the game is **constant-sum**. Therefore, finding a Nash equilibrium is equivalent to solving a constant-sum game.

However, the payoffs are not symmetric between players.

So Player 1 wants to maximize their own expected payoff, given that Player 2 is also choosing optimally.

- If your number is **greater**, you get Y − 2 → **worse than matching**, because Y < X ⇒ Y−2 < X

This suggests:

### **Step 5: Find optimal mixed strategy for Player 1**

We are to find a strategy (i.e., probabilities over A0, A1, A2, A3) such that Player 1 maximizes their **expected payoff**, assuming Player 2 is also playing optimally (possibly in symmetric or asymmetric way).

Since the game is symmetric in structure (same rules for both players), and both are rational, we might look for a **symmetric Nash equilibrium**.

But note: the payoffs are **not symmetric between players**, so equilibrium may not be symmetric.

However, in many such games, the **minimax equilibrium** (which maximizes the worst-case expected payoff) is the solution.

But we can look for a mixed strategy where **Player 1 is indifferent among all actions that are played with positive probability**.

Let’s suppose that in equilibrium, Player 1 plays a distribution (p0, p1, p2, p3) over A0, A1, A2, A3, and Player 2 also plays some strategy (q0, q1, q2, q3), and at equilibrium, both are playing best responses.

But since we’re only choosing our own strategy (and assuming the other player is rational and will play optimally), we should find a **minimax strategy** for Player 1.

Alternatively, assume that in equilibrium, both players use the same strategy (symmetric equilibrium). Then we can solve for symmetric mixed strategy.

Let’s test whether such a symmetric equilibrium exists.

Assume Player 2 uses strategy (q₀, q₁, q₂, q₃) = (p₀, p₁, p₂, p₃), and we want to find p₀ + p₁ + p₂ + p₃ = 1 such that no player can profitably deviate.

That is, the expected payoff of any action used (with positive probability) should be equal, and no action outside the support should give higher expected payoff.

So we’ll compute expected payoff for each action **against the distribution (p₀, p₁, p₂, p₃)**, and set them equal for all actions in support.

Let’s suppose that **all four actions are played with positive probability** in equilibrium.

Let the unknown probabilities be:
- p0 = Pr(A0)
- p1 = Pr(A1)
- p2 = Pr(A2)
- p3 = Pr(A3)
- p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

The expected payoff for Player 1 playing A0 is:

E0 = p0×2 + p1×4 + p2×4 + p3×4  
= 2p0 + 4(p1 + p2 + p3)  
= 2p0 + 4(1 − p0)  
= 2p0 + 4 − 4p0  
= 4 − 2p0

Similarly, expected payoff for A1:

E1 = p0×0 + p1×3 + p2×5 + p3×5  
= 0p0 + 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3

= 3p1 + 5(p2 + p3)

Expected payoff for A2:

E2 = p0×0 + p1×1 + p2×4 + p3×6  
= 1p1 + 4p2 + 6p3

Expected payoff for A3:

E3 = p0×0 + p1×1 + p2×2 + p3×5  
= 1p1 + 2p2 + 5p3

In symmetric equilibrium, all E0 = E1 = E2 = E3 = v (some value).

So:
(1) E0 = 4 − 2p0 = v  
(2) E1 = 3p1 + 5p2 + 5p3 = v  
(3) E2 = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3 = v  
(4) E3 = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3 = v  
(5) p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

Now solve step by step.

From (1): v = 4 − 2p0 → p0 = (4 − v)/2

From (4): v = p1 + 2p2 + 5p3  
From (3): v = p1 + 4p2 + 6p3  
Subtract (4) from (3):  
0 = (p1 + 4p2 + 6p3) − (p1 + 2p2 + 5p3) = 2p2 + p3  
→ 2p2 + p3 = 0  

But p2, p3 ≥ 0 → only solution is p2 = p3 = 0

Then from (4): v = p1 + 0 + 0 = p1  
From (3): v = p1 → consistent

From (2): v = 3p1 + 5×0 + 5×0 = 3p1 → so v = 3p1

But v = p1 from (4) → so 3p1 = p1 → 2p1 = 0 → p1 = 0 → v = 0

Then p1 = 0, p2 = 0, p3 = 0 → p0 = 1

So only solution is p0 = 1, p1 = p2 = p3 = 0

But then v = 4 − 2×1 = 2

But from other equations:
- E1 = 3×0 + ... = 0 ≠ 2
- So not consistent.

Contradiction.

Therefore, **our assumption that all four actions are used** is false.

So in symmetric equilibrium, not all actions are played.

Let’s try a smaller support.

Now, recall that in the earlier attempt with only A0 and A3, A2 was a better response.

But what if we find a strategy that makes A2 not profitable?

Perhaps try to see what mixed strategy makes Player 1 indifferent between A0 and A2.

But we need a different approach.

Let’s try to solve for a strategy that makes Player 2 indifferent, so that Player 1 can exploit.

But since we are Player 1, we want to choose a distribution that maximizes our **minimum possible payoff** — the minimax strategy.

Let’s try to find a probability distribution for Player 1 such that against **any** strategy of Player 2, our expected payout is at least some value v, and we maximize v.

By minimax theorem, this gives the value of the game.

Assume Player 1 uses (p0, p1, p2, p3)

We want to maximize v such that for all pure strategies of Player 2, the expected payoff of Player 1 is at least v.

So:
Against A0: E = p0*2 + p1*0 + p2*0 + p3*0 = 2p0 ≥ v
Against A1: E = p0*4 + p1*3 + p2*1 + p3*1 = 4p0 + 3p1 + p2 + p3 ≥ v
Against A2: E = p0*4 + p1*5 + p2*4 + p3*2 = 4p0 + 5p1 + 4p2 + 2p3 ≥ v
Against A3: E = p0*4 + p1*5 + p2*6 + p3*5 = 4p0 + 5p1 + 6p2 + 5p3 ≥ v

And p0 + p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

We want to maximize v subject to:
1. 2p0 ≥ v
2. 4p0 + 3p1 + p2 + p3 ≥ v
3. 4p0 + 5p1 + 4p2 + 2p3 ≥ v
4. 4p0 + 5p1 + 6p2 + 5p3 ≥ v
5. p0+...+p3 = 1

Let’s assume equality in the binding constraints.

Suppose that the worst-case payoff is from pure A0, so 2p0 = v

Then p0 = v/2

Also, the other constraints must hold.

Let’s try to set p0 = v/2, and assume that the other constraints are tight.

But there are four.

Try to see if A3 is the most threatening.

Or try numerical values.

Try p0 = 0. then from (1): 0 ≥ v → v ≤ 0

Not good.

Try p0 = 0.2 → v ≤ 0.4

Then try to make all other expressions at least 0.4

But let’s try to assume symmetry or guess.

From the matrix, it seems that if you play A0 often, you are safe against A0, but may be exploited by A1.

But if you play A2, you can get 6 if opponent plays A3, but if they play A0, you get 0.

So you want to mix to avoid being exploited.

Let’s try to find a strategy where the expected payoff is the same against A0, A1, A2, A3.

We want:
2p0 = 4p0 + 3p1 + p2 + p3 = 4p0 + 5p1 + 4p2 + 2p3 = 4p0 + 5p1 + 6p2 + 5p3

Let’s denote the first as v.

So:
(1) 2p0 = v
(2) 4p0 + 3p1 + p2 + p3 = v
(3) 4p0 + 5p1 + 4p2 + 2p3 = v
(4) 4p0 + 5p1 + 6p2 + 5p3 = v

From (1): p0 = v/2

From (2):
4*(v/2) + 3p1 + p2 + p3 = v
2v + 3p1 + p2 + p3 = v
3p1 + p2 + p3 = -v

But left side ≥0, right side <0 if v>0 — impossible.

So cannot have all payoff equal.

So the first constraint cannot be satisfied with equality if we want v>0.

Instead, perhaps the binding constraint is not A0, but others.

Suppose that the minimax strategy makes Player 1 indifferent between A1, A2, A3, and not caring about A0.

But A0 is safe.

After research, recall that in such games, the equilibrium often involves playing the lowest action with high probability.

Let’s try a guess: play A0 with high probability, to avoid the risk of being beaten.

Try: play A0 with 50%, A2 with 50%

Then p0 = 0.5, p2 = 0.5, p1=p3=0

Compute expected payoff against each pure strategy of Player 2:

- vs A0: 0.5*2 + 0.5*0 = 1
- vs A1: 0.5*4 + 0.5*1 = 2 + 0.5 = 2.5
- vs A2: 0.5*4 + 0.5*4 = 2 + 2 = 4
- vs A3: 0.5*4 + 0.5*6 = 2 + 3 = 5

So worst-case is 1 (when opponent plays A0), which is low.

Not good.

Try: play A2 with 100% → worst-case when opponent plays A0: gets 0

Bad.

Play A0 with 100%: if opponent plays A0, get 2; if A1, get 4; if A2, get 4; if A3, get 4. So minimum is 2.

So min payoff = 2.

Can we do better than that?

Try to make min payoff > 2.

Suppose we play A0: p0 = 0.6, A1: p1 = 0.4, others 0

Then:
- vs A0: 0.6*2 + 0.4*0 = 1.2
- vs A1: 0.6*4 + 0.4*3 = 2.4 + 1.2 = 3.6
- vs A2: 0.6*4 + 0.4*5 = 2.4 + 2.0 = 4.4
- vs A3: 0.6*4 + 0.4*5 = 4.4

Worst-case: 1.2 < 2 — worse than pure A0.

 try A0: 0.8, A2: 0.2

- vs A0: 0.8*2 + 0.2*0 = 1.6
- vs A1: 0.8*4 + 0.2*1 = 3.2 + 0.2 = 3.4
- vs A2: 0.8*4 + 0.2*4 = 3.2 + 0.8 = 4.0
- vs A3: 0.8*4 + 0.2*6 = 3.2 + 1.2 = 4.4

Worst-case: 1.6 < 2

So pure A0 gives min 2, and any mix with other actions reduces the payoff against A0.

Therefore, to maximize the minimum payoff, we should play only A0.

Then min payoff is 2, and it's constant.

But is there a strategy with min payoff > 2?

Suppose we play only A0.

Then against A0: 2
A1: 4
A2: 4
A3: 4

So min is 2.

Can we get min > 2?

Suppose we play A1 only: then against A0: gets 0 < 2

A2 only: against A0: 0 < 2

A3 only: against A0: 0 < 2

Any mix with A1, A2, A3 will have at least one opponent action that makes payoff < 2 when it's against A0.

For example, if you play A0 with 0.7 and A1 with 0.3, then against A0: 0.7*2 + 0.3*0 = 1.4 < 2

So only way to have min payoff >= 2 is if the expected payoff against A0 is at least 2.

Against A0: only A0 gives 2, A1, A2, A3 give 0,0,0.

So to have E against A0 >= 2, must have p0 >= 1, because otherwise 2p0 < 2.

2p0 >= 2 → p0 >= 1 → p0 = 1.

Therefore, the only strategy with min payoff >= 2 is p0=1.

And for any other strategy, min payoff < 2.

Therefore, the minimax strategy is to play A0 with 100%.

And the value of the game is 2.

Moreover, if you play A0 with 100%, the other player cannot get more than 2 if they are also rational, but in worst case, they can get 2, but they might choose other actions.

But since we are to maximize our expected points, and if we play A0, we get at least 2, and at risk of not being beaten.

But is there a better strategy?

Suppose we play A0 with 100%, then we get 2 if they play A0, 4 if they play higher.

So average if they mix, we get more than 2.

But the worst-case (against A0) is 2.

And we cannot do better in worst-case.

So playing A0 with 100% is the best worst-case strategy.

Moreover, if the other player is rational, they might not play A0, but they might.

So to maximize guaranteed payoff, A0 is best.

Therefore, the optimal strategy is to play A0 with 100%.

Final answer:

{"A0": 100, "A1": 0, "A2": 0, "A3": 0}
